ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u2	Mr Vice-President, honourable Members and Members, colleagues and colleagues of the Government! Today on the agenda of the National Assembly is an important topic, perhaps more important than we are aware of it, given the relatively dry title - the statement of guidelines and so on. Usually, something very attractive is not hidden under such titles, but this time it seems to me that it is a very important topic for several reasons. In this introduction, I will try to further substantiate this. Allow me to say that, given that it is quite a detail in the text itself, what we consider to be the key directions for Slovenia's functioning in the institutions of the European Union and that it probably does not make sense to repeat this in a long and broad way - if there are any dilemmas, we will do so later when we explain it in specific areas. By allowing me to try to give a more general picture of the situation in which the European Union is located today, the situation of Slovenia within this picture, and I am also trying to point out the opportunities offered for us in this situation. For the current situation in which the European Union is located, we could say that the following are significant. Firstly, the institutional crisis, which is relatively serious following the rejection of the ratification of the European Constitutional Treaty. Secondly, the current situation in which the European Union is located is characterised by the fact that the institutions of the Union are still operating, that there is no blockade, that once agreement has been reached on the next financial perspective, it has become clear that the institutional crisis will not develop into a general crisis that will undermine the functioning of the European Union. It is precisely this step that the Union has shown that it can be effective when dealing with current issues in the enlarged configuration. For the current situation of the European Union, there are also dilemmas in the implementation of the Lisbon Strategy, which we are practically renovating at least partially every year, and this year we can say for the first time that, after a long time, years of relative stagnation, including the decline in economic growth, we are recording positive trends and, on the basis of these trends, the recently concluded European Council, the European Summit has also expressed more optimistic expectations about the millions of new jobs that can be created in the coming period and also in terms of overall economic growth. At this point, the European Union is also facing some of the issues related to the functioning of the single market because of the above-mentioned issues. The ambitions that existed here to extend this market to free movement of services have greatly decreased since the European Parliament first completed reading of the Services Directive and these initial ambitions by practically igniting the approach in terms of taking account of the country of destination rather than of the country of origin in service providers has significantly reduced. We also face the dilemma of the Single Market in the field of energy, because there have also been partial steps in the last European Council, although the decision on the liberalisation of the energy market in the European Union has already been taken in 2002 and it is now only for the commitments made to be realised. And the current situation in which the European Union is located is also characterised by the dilemmas related to the further enlargement of this European Union, at this time there is no general agreement on any further steps. The decisions to start negotiations with Croatia and Turkey have already been made with a number of dilemmas, and today there are no uniform interpretations of some of the formulations adopted by the European Council when it adopted both decisions. For steps that need to be taken further in order for the situation as it is to move in a productive direction, it is necessary to make some important or important decisions that can only be followed in the right order. Before I say anything about this sequence, it is necessary to be a little more hasty about the causes that have led to the current situation, particularly the present institutional crisis, as expressed in the deadlock, this is the ratification of the European Constitutional Treaty. The European Constitutional Treaty was launched in order to ensure the effective functioning of the European Union in new situations, mainly caused by historical expansion after the fall of the Berlin Wall, as well as in situations where the world is increasingly global, when the overall and economic competitiveness at this level is exacerbated and when the European Union can only be successful if it is more integrated and externally acting in a uniform manner. However, this process of preparation and subsequent ratification of the European Constitutional Treaty coincided with some phenomena which had a strong impact on public opinion mainly in the Member States of the European Union or in the vast number of EU countries 15. First of all, the effects of globalisation have already been mentioned. These impacts directly endangered jobs in traditional industries, in the European Union countries, and there was some general uncertainty and fear for their own jobs, which slowly grew into, say, a sense of uncertainty that had an important impact on the conduct of individuals even when it came to voting on the European Constitutional Treaty. Then came the enlargement, which has been treated differently in countries that have entered the Union and in countries that have already been there, or citizens of the founding Member States of the European Union, and also some others have a different attitude towards the European Union, which is understandable. They are born in this space, in a space of freedom, prosperity, relatively great opportunities, high standards, protection of human rights. To them, this space is largely self-evident. In the countries or countries where we have recently joined the European Union, this is not the case. Therefore, it was also necessary to decide, not least at the referendum. The attitude towards the Union is much more emotional than self-evident, and this is a strategic breakthrough in terms of views, and it is not a coincidence that the blockade or rejection of the European Constitutional Treaty in the two countries that are the founding members of the European Union, France and the Netherlands, where these fears, caused first by negative effects or the alleged negative effects of globalisation, have been built up with the fears of people in these countries before the loss of their jobs, which would be threatened by the valuable labour force that will now come from Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia, in short, from the east and with the sense that the European Union, with these new powers it receives through the European Constitutional Treaty, does not constitute any mechanism to protect this situation, this situation or this prosperity, but rather a threat earlier. This feeling was also accompanied by fears caused by terrorist attacks in London, earlier in Madrid, with political killings, with specific motives in the Netherlands, with social unrest mainly in the emigrant communities in France and so on. This all created a new atmosphere that covered optimism that prevailed in Europe after the fall of the Berlin Wall, after some old, long-standing borders and barriers fell. This situation has had an important impact on the decision of individuals invited to a referendum to vote on whether the EU should for the first time in history be given its constitution or constitutional treaty. In these votes, particularly in France, in the Netherlands, which were negative, it would probably be negative in another country if this series of referendums were followed as planned. The votes against were not so conditioned by the dissatisfaction with the text on which it was voted, or by the institutions on the basis of this text, but rather by the fears I have been talking about, because security is a key asset for people living in freedom. Then there's prosperity, prosperity, and so on. And when people feel that their safety is threatened, they react by rejecting decisions that they feel will not increase or deepen their integrity. And from the EU, as some supranational institutions, most voters in the Netherlands or France simply did not expect a solution, but rather a deepening of the problem. This is why, in order to overcome this blockade and this institutional crisis, it is most important to restore confidence in the EU, especially where it is most threatened or shaken. And this is mostly not the new Member States, but it is the founding members of the EU who, for various reasons that I will not mention, also have a specific weight slightly different, and it is clear that there will be no further steps without movement at this point. In order to restore this confidence, it is very important that the EU ensure further growth, the creation of new jobs, all that creates a general sense of social security and general security. It is important that the European Union effectively tackles illegal immigration, and it is no coincidence that so much space is devoted to it. Only after this trust has been restored can new solutions be sought or the procedures for ratifying the European Constitutional Treaty, which have now come to an end. I firmly believe that it is not possible to do this this year or at the next European summit, even though there will be a debate on this at that time. Earlier, it is necessary to provide some general conditions for this movement to occur in thoughtfulness and that people will actually feel safe again and that they will trust that, by giving new power to European institutions, they do not increase risks, but that these risks are lower. It is also very important that a positive approach to the general trends of globalisation, which are not only a threat, is also a new challenge. Globalisation also offers many new opportunities, and the European Union can take advantage of them for the common good, including for the common good of all citizens of the Member States, by addressing these challenges in order to make them more competitive in this field and thus, in the end, to mitigate the negative effects of globalisation. So the first step that it takes to make this situation move faster in a positive direction is to restore trust. Restoring confidence is only possible if economic growth increases if new jobs are created, if young people are able to get to life opportunities faster than on average today; if the European Union is able to significantly better and more quickly organise the transfer of good practices from those Member States where things work, to those countries where they face dilemmas in different areas. Well, next step, in third place. Once we have regained confidence and made some institutional moves, the next step may be further expansion. There will be no major further enlargement of the European Union until this institutional crisis is resolved. In this situation in which the European Union is located, Slovenia, as one of the 25, is in short one of the 27 members. The macroeconomic figures show that Slovenia is in a good position. We meet the very strict conditions laid down by the Stability and Growth Pact. Since November last year, we have also met the conditions for entry into the European Monetary Union. By the end of March 2006, we are the only candidate for entry into the euro area that actually meets all the conditions, the big question being Estonia, as one of the candidates, has already given up this ambition for the following year, the big question is whether Lithuania will be able to reverse the negative inflation trends it has now. In short, Slovenia can be satisfied here, in these comparisons. We can really expect to meet the conditions for entry into the Schengen area next year in October, which will mean that Slovenia is the thirteenth Member State to join this more integrated internal circle of the European Union. This is very important. With these steps, Slovenia will somehow step out now, in order to meet the conditions, when they are actually made, also completely formal Slovenia will somehow withdraw from its current status when one of the ten new members joins, let's say, this internal, more integrated core of the European Union and, of course, this brings new opportunities. Some are themselves when we go to the euro area, to the Schengen area, and others are, of course, conditional that we can take advantage of if we are well prepared for them and can be largely delayed if we do not. The fact is that Slovenia, with the current level of development, is more than 80% of the European Union's average development, is also above some of the old Member States. By maintaining some internal stability, some continuity of economic and social development, in order to successfully modernise within the framework of economic and social reforms, we can substantially upgrade these trends. It is very important that we also establish ourselves as a 100% legal state, according to the household. Here our main problem is the many judicial backlogs that cast a bad shadow on this position of ours. This must be taken seriously, with the opportunity we are given on this basis in 2008 when Slovenia, as the first new member, will preside over the European Union, we can round this positive picture very nicely. And after 2008, we can actually cross, both by numerical indicators and by some general image from the news circle to the circle of established members of the European Union, as a country that is capable not only of providing some continuous, positive development for its own citizens, but is also able to generate good practices for other Member States. At the end, I would stress the fact that, because of such a development, because we do not have any problems with the criteria of the Stability and Growth Pact, nor with the criteria for entry into the European Monetary Union, I hope that the same assessment will be made for the entry into Schengen next year. This makes Slovenia a positive example in the EU today, which the EU needs very much. It is precisely because of the state of some broken trust, because of the fear that many employees in the EU's founding Member States will be threatened by workers who are willing to work for half or for a third of their salary, as well as by the other feelings of some fear of the uncertainty that enlargement and the enlarged EU are supposed to bring. As a result, Slovenia, as an example of a country that does not present this real or perceived threat to jobs, is already reaching or exceeding 80% of European development, catch-up or speeding up some of the old EU or EU Member States 15. Slovenia can, for all these reasons, be cited as a very positive example, as something that emerges from some, unfortunately, widely distributed images among the ordinary citizens of some EU countries that emerge from some simplified patterns of how it will be necessary for new members, for the poorer East to pay even for a long generation and for future generations. Slovenia can successfully break these stereotypes with its own example, in its position, with its own development. To the extent that we are able to upgrade this with the successful Presidency, which we are preparing intensively, then we can say that we have been an important stone in the mosaic of building new confidence, which the EU needs very much with us to take steps from these institutional blockades in which it is, and to take decisions that will allow further growth, job creation and further expansion. Further enlargement of the EU is, in my opinion, a strategic imperative. And speaking of this step, I am not just referring to the Western Balkan countries, which are our priorities because of our proximity and other strategic interests, and I am also talking about the wider EU neighbourhood. There are certainly reflections on the issue of energy supply for Europe and the issue of natural resources, as well as geostrategic rounding, that are very important. And it is in the interest of the European Union that this area is expanding, that this space of stability does not end sharply at the borders of the European Union, but that these are soft transitions and that these soft transitions in real time, when those who aspire to membership in the European Union meet the conditions, are also upgraded by the actual enlargement of the European Union or in conjunction with certain privileged statuses, so that some space of stability, prosperity and progress is actually broadened. Only after we actually create real conditions for this process after this current deadlock, in which we have been given a similar span or at least about the same scale - probably the same due to historical circumstances - will it never - as at the time the European Union was expanding with the wave we were part of, will we be confident that the European Union, as a whole, will actually be able to participate equally in the globalisation competition with the spaces, with the environment that is evolving more rapidly than we are today; but in many areas at a significantly lower initial level, so what big panic is not needed here, but serious and strategic reflection is needed. With this declaration that you have before you today, I think we are adopting the right guidelines for the functioning of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union. Many of the proposals in this text go beyond the ambitions of this year, and things will continue in the coming years, for sure. Of course, it is our interest to upgrade all that we do successfully in one year with successful steps also in the future and that there is a continuity of good work and good practice. Given that we have a relatively fresh practice, fresh experience, even when it comes to cooperation between government services or between the institutions of the executive and parliament in coordinating these steps, given that sometimes these deadlines are very short, and given that many things are being discussed, decided or codecisiond in Brussels, we will probably have to revise the techniques of this cooperation, coordination, a little reduction of this ballast or burden, a procedure where it is too long or unnecessary, and ensure a faster flow of information where important decisions are indeed concerned, as well as coordination and decision-making. However, I think that, for the time being, this cooperation has also gone hand in hand with the fact that there were no important obstacles, that at least some of the good practices that had been established at the time when Slovenia was still negotiating for membership in the European Union could be built up, and the government wants things to be done in this direction in the future. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u3	Thank you very much, Mr. President. I give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr Dimitri Ruple. Here you go, please.
ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u4	Mr President-in-Office. Colleagues, Members of Parliament, Members of Parliament. Allow me to add a few words myself to the external aspects of the declaration of guidelines for the functioning of our country in the institutions of the European Union in 2006. First of all, I'm going to say something that is probably familiar to everyone. Slovenia is a member of the European Union and the European Union is a global player, and this, of course, means that Slovenia is itself in some way a global player. Both Slovenia and the European Union have come a long way in recent years. Today, the European Union is able to respond to crises and instability not only in the European continent, but wherever in the world, the latest decision to participate in the Congo also confirms. Slovenia also plays an active and successful role in the external political field in the European Union. With its external political activity, the European Union has made an important contribution to strengthening global stability, security, peace and development. The Western Balkan countries are getting closer to European integration, the deepening of relations with the European neighbourhood countries has continued, the European Union has been actively involved in the reconstruction and stabilisation of Iraq, has played an important role in the Middle East peace process, has made progress in relations with the United States of America, China and other partners. Nevertheless, we must be aware that the European Union is still halfway there. Our ability to respond to changes in the international environment must be even faster and even more effective. The European Union has played an important role when, on the outside, it has acted unitedly and with public views and Slovenia has so far been distinguished by its contribution to this unity or by its failure to break it. In this regard, it is particularly important that the debate on the future of the European Union continues in 2006; something has already been said about this; let me say just a few more words about this. The ability of the European Union to act coherently externally depends largely on its effective internal structure. For Slovenia and, of course, for most members of the European Union, including Europe in a broader sense, the Treaty of Constitution is of paramount importance for Europe. It represents the broadest possible consensus on the future institutional structure of the European Union, without which some key projects, such as enlargement, would be compromised. The country will continue to participate actively in the debate on the future of the European Union and will form it as soon as possible in 2008. We will take a thoughtful and pragmatic approach, the Constitutional Treaty is of course important, because it enables the European Union to be more effective or should enable the European Union to be as effective as possible, as simple as possible, as a European Union that is as understandable as possible to contribute to the security and well-being of all citizens. The period of reflection, as it is called, is slowly running out and the period of intense search for solutions begins. But there's something clear now. Any solution will have to have the support of the citizens of the European Union. I would like to remind you that there is a debate in the institutions of the European Union on how to explain to the public, especially in the old Member States, how these citizens can be explained the essence of European integration. The essence of European integration, European integration suddenly today, at a time when Slovenia has become a member, or when the membership of the European Union has increased, is no longer self-evident. Integration for the European Union is no longer self-evident. That is why we must make every effort, perhaps even in the parliaments of all countries, to bring the Constitution and future issues as close to the citizens as possible. We will, of course, have to work intensively with the Constitution and with the issues of the future of Europe during our presidency. The debate on the future of the European Union is also closely linked to the debate on further enlargement. Slovenia will also advocate a positive accession of the European Union to enlargement in 2006. It is crucial that enlargement policy remains high on the agenda of the European Union, as it represents one of its most powerful instruments in achieving the progress of candidate countries and potential candidates. This will be the most important approximation of the Western Balkan countries for us. Of course, all countries that have a European perspective and are at a certain stage of European integration must respect and meet certain political and economic criteria and act in accordance with common European standards and values. Slovenia is prepared to assist these countries in meeting these objectives. To this end, they have also set up a centre to help bring the European Union closer. For example, Bulgaria and Romania are expected to become members of the European Union on 1 January 2007, it is said very soon. In this context, the progress report presented by the European Commission in May will be crucial. We will also closely monitor Turkey's progress in the negotiating process. Slovenia supports Croatia's membership in the European Union. It also sees the approach to negotiations as a framework within which solutions can be found for open bilateral issues in the European spirit. The speed of Croatia's accession negotiations will depend in particular on Croatia's readiness to take over, implement and comply with European legislation and European standards. In these negotiations, Croatia will also be able to demonstrate its credibility with full respect for what has been agreed. There's only one other observation. Slovenia and 9 other new members were more fortunate than some of our neighbours or Western Balkan countries. We have come to the European Union at a wave of energy triggered by the fall of the Berlin Wall. The Republic of Slovenia has arrived in the European Union at the right time, and today this energy appears to be exhausted and may even be consumed. Slovenia, if I look back a moment ago, as Chair of the Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe, has devoted much of its time to an in-depth dialogue with countries that fall within the framework of European Neighbourhood Policy. This year, within the framework of the European Union, we will try to use our knowledge and actively contribute to the upgrading of relations between the European Union and those countries. We will advocate the active implementation of the action plans already adopted and the early preparation of new ones. Controversial cartoons, mainly linked to publications in Danish newspapers and outbreaks of violence, which testify that the international community, or if you want the European Union, is under-devoting to the dialogue of civilizations and cultures. To effectively address and resolve conflict situations in the world, mutual understanding and tolerance are essential, understanding and tolerance and the only possible path to lasting stability. We will continue to encourage the Barcelona process, in particular the Analint Foundation, for dialogue between cultures and civilizations, in order to be more effective in dialogue between partners, and thus achieve mutual understanding and respect. Finally, we need dialogue between religions and churches in Slovenia. With the emergence of new global players, such as India and China, the international environment is changing, so it will be extremely important to continue with the chapter of the Transatlantic Partnership and at the same time develop an effective strategy for relations with incoming world powers. The issue of energy appears to be one of the key issues in the international community in the coming period. The Secretary-General of Solana has been tasked with drawing up a specific foreign policy strategy on this issue. The Spring European Council was also intended to develop an energy policy for Europe. In this light, relations between the European Union and Russia will have a new dynamic and context. The Republic of Slovenia will continue to advocate dialogue with Russia. This should be based on equality and consideration of common interests. This dialogue will be somewhat complicated because of the events we have witnessed in these days, Ukraine and Belarus, in Transnistria and for the sake of communication on the other hand, between Russia and China. The year 2006 will also be important for development policy. Within this, the European Union will continue its active policy towards Africa. The implementation of the adopted European Union strategy for Africa last December is our joint support to the African countries to emerge from the scourge of poverty and to take a firm path towards achieving the Millennium Development Goals. Discussions within the European Union will aim not only at sound development policies but also at the coherence of trade, environment, migration, agricultural and other policies. At the outset, I have already mentioned the growing activity of the European Union in the global environment. This is particularly evident in the conduct of the European Union, which also takes over and runs crisis and civilian operations. The last such important decision is the operation already mentioned in the Congo. The speed and non-problem nature of this decision is of paramount importance to the decision-making process in the European Union. Slovenia will continue to support the European Union within the common European defence policy, which will be willing and able to assume its responsibility for conducting crisis operations. In doing so, transparent cooperation and coordination with NATO will be particularly important. Even now, we can say that 2006 will be extremely varied and dynamic in the foreign policy area. Our country will continue to pursue its objectives and priorities within the European Union and at the same time actively cooperate in strengthening the common foreign and security policy that will help resolve conflict situations in the world and develop peaceful relations between countries. If I conclude, among other tasks that I have already mentioned and explained in more detail, we will also engage in regional integration within our European mission and our European mission. We have before us the creation of a euro area, we will deal with policy towards neighbouring countries and we will prepare intensively for the Presidency of the European Union, which will start in early 2008. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u6	Dear Prime Minister, ladies and gentlemen of the Ministers, the distinguished representative of the Government's Service for European Affairs, honourable Members and Members! At its 42nd meeting, on 17 March 2006, the Committee on European Union Affairs considered the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for its consideration and acceptance. At its meeting, the European Union Affairs Committee also took note of the report on the situation in the European Union and the situation of the Republic of Slovenia in 2005. Within a time limit set by 15 March 2006, the amendments to the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union were tabled in 2006 by the following proposers, the home working bodies and Members: the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy of the two amendments, the Committee on Economic Affairs of the seven amendments, the Committee on Agriculture, Forestry and Food of the two amendments, and Ambassador Ferri Horvat of the two amendments. To these amendments, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, 17.3.2006, forwarded to the Committee a written position in which it supported all amendments and made comments on two. The Committee on the Affairs of the European Union has taken these comments into account and supported the amendments. The opening presentation of the proposal for a declaration was presented by Katja Rejec Longar, Deputy Director of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for European Affairs, and by Dr Janez Shumrad, v.d. Director-General of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs for European Affairs and Political Bilateral. Both presented the stronger political priorities of their work circle. At the same time, the Government's European Affairs Service said that the three key political priorities are to take over the euro, establish the European Union's external border and free movement of people and implement the Lisbon Strategy. The representative of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, however, presented Slovenia's narrower foreign policy priorities. In the debate, the members and members of the Committee asked representatives of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia a few questions and made comments. Chapter 11 - The environment was given a positive opinion or endorsement by the members of the Committee. Chapter 9 - The information society has been exposed to the fact that Slovenia's priorities are poorly processed, but in fact they represent the actual situation. The members of the Committee also thanked the Government for their cooperation in the consideration of the proposal for a declaration on the home work bodies, then discussed how the substantive preparations of the Republic of Slovenia for the EU Presidency were being carried out and how they were affected by the absence of the State Secretary of the Government's European Affairs Service. The debate also took place on how the Government of the Republic of Slovenia will promote and implement Slovenian as one of the official and constructive languages of the EU and as a means of promoting Slovenia itself, how Slovenia and Slovenian culture will be recognised in the EU, and what role it will play in this area until and during the EU Presidency. The debate also took place on the position of the Republic of Slovenia on the planned amendments to the directive on television without borders, which is likely to be adopted in 2008. The members of the committee expressed support and joy that the establishment of the European Institute for Gender Equality has become a project of Slovenia, which is also being launched in Slovenia, and that funds are also envisaged in the budget of the Republic of Slovenia. The representative of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia provided the following answers to the questions raised and comments raised. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia has been preparing for the presidency since 1.1.2005. The work of the Government's European Affairs Service under the leadership of the Deputy Secretary of State shall be carried out smoothly. Preparations for the Presidency of Slovenia are under way. To this end, a special working group has been set up, consisting of representatives from all ministries, and will be coordinated by a representative of the Government's European Affairs Service. The staffing structure for assistance in the Presidency has already been established, as there is a list of people who will participate. The EU Presidency programme of the Republic of Slovenia will be presented by the new Head of Government for European Affairs in May. As part of its consideration of the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the EU institutions in 2006, the Committee adopted 20 amendments. The EU Affairs Committee has also proposed its amendments and adopted them without further discussion. The amendments referred to the following chapters: the opening part, the continuation of preparations for the adoption of the euro, the third chapter - the Constitutional Treaty, the fourth chapter - General and institutional matters, the sixth chapter - the General Economic Framework or Policy Coordination, the seventh chapter - Strengthening competitiveness, the eleventh chapter - Environment, the thirteenth chapter - the Common Agricultural Policy and Fisheries, and the sixteenth chapter - Foreign Policy. At the meeting, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia orally identified and supported the amendments adopted. On 22.3. the government also sent a written opinion on the amendments of the competent working bodies and supported them. The amendments adopted are included in the proposal for a completed declaration, after the discussion and the vote on the amendments, the Committee voted on the updated proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the EU institutions in 2006 and Slovenia's priorities for the work of the Council of the EU in 2005 and adopted the following decision: The EU Affairs Committee adopted at its 42nd regular meeting on 17.3.2006 a supplementary proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the EU in 2006.
ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u7	Thank you very much. I give the floor to the Vice-President of the Committee on Foreign Policy, Mr Jožet Horvat, to present the report of the Committee. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u8	Thank you for saying so, Mr Vice President. Dear Prime Minister Janša, distinguished ministers with colleagues, colleagues and colleagues! At its 12th regular meeting, on 17 March this year, the Committee on Foreign Policy, pursuant to Article 154(1) of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia, considered as a competent working body the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union in 2006 with a communication on the situation in the European Union and the situation of the Republic of Slovenia in it in 2005, which, pursuant to the second paragraph of Article 5 of the Law on cooperation between the National Assembly and the Government in matters of the European Union and the second paragraph 154.f and the first paragraph of Article 154. of the Rules of Procedure, submitted to the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia for information or for consideration and acceptance of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia. In its opening remarks, Committee President Jožef Jerovšek said that for the third time the National Assembly had been considering the proposal for a declaration as provided for by the law on cooperation between the Government and the National Assembly on matters of the European Union and the Rules of Procedure. In accordance with Rule 154 of the Rules of Procedure, the Committee on Foreign Policy will carry out an examination of the proposal for a declaration in the field of foreign and security policy and the enlargement of the European Union, adding that it has also received a report on the situation in the European Union and the situation of the Republic of Slovenia in 2005. President Jerovšek also pointed out that the proposal for a declaration as a parent working body in the field of security policy had already been addressed by the Defence Committee, which had comments on the document. Mr Rupel, Minister for Foreign Affairs, stressed that the Republic of Slovenia had established itself as a credible and active member during the two-year period of membership in the European Union and that the Presidency of the European Union would pose a particular challenge for Slovenia in the coming years. He said that the declaration sets Slovenia's priorities in the field of European affairs in 2006 and covers wider and narrower political priorities in specific areas. He presented the key content of that part of the document dealing with foreign and security policy and these are the preparation for the Presidency of the European Union, the future of the European Union, the enlargement of the European Union and the Western Balkan countries, the European Neighbourhood Policy and the Barcelona process, the strengthening of the role of the European Union in the global international community and development assistance. With regard to the Presidency of the European Union, Minister Rupel stressed that Slovenia is preparing for a self-presidency presidency, which is likely to be based on the 18-month Presidency programme, which Slovenia will develop together with Germany and Portugal, and should also start talks with France, which will chair in the second half of 2008. With regard to the future of the European Union, Minister Rupel said that the Treaty of Constitution for Europe is of exceptional importance for the European Union, as important European projects will be threatened without institutional changes to the existing treaties. With regard to the discussions on the general enlargement strategy of the European Union, Slovenia remains committed to keeping enlargement policy high on the agenda of the European Union. Slovenia thus supports the entry of Bulgaria and Romania into the European Union in January 2007, provided that the required criteria are met. Slovenia supports Croatia's membership in the European Union and sees accession to the negotiations as a framework within which solutions can be found for open bilateral issues in the European spirit. Slovenia will also closely monitor accession negotiations with Turkey and Macedonia. Minister Rupel also drew attention to the importance of the European Union's neighbourhood policy towards countries that are at the very border of the European Union and want closer cooperation with it. The Barcelona Process will also be extremely important, particularly as a dialogue between cultures and civilizations, which actively promotes mutual understanding and respect. It is also important to strengthen the role of the European Union in the global international community, where it will have to become more effective as it becomes a big, global partner. In the area of development cooperation, the attention of the European Union will also be focused in 2006 on Africa, in particular on poverty eradication and conflict resolution. After a short discussion, the Committee voted on the amendments to the Committee. With 12 votes and no votes against, the Committee adopted the amendment to Chapter 3.1, enlargement of the European Union and of the Western Balkans and 13 votes in favour and without vote against the amendment of the Committee to Chapter 16.1 of the Barcelona Process. The amendments adopted are included, together with the amendments of the Committee on European Union Affairs, in a joint proposal for a declaration, which is an integral part of both reports. After the discussion and the vote on the amendments, the Committee voted on the proposal for a declaration on guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union in 2006 and supported it by 12 votes to none. Thank you for your attention.

In the DeSUS Group, we will support the proposal for a declaration on guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union in 2006, with a report on the situation in the EU and the situation of the Republic of Slovenia in 2005. The proposal for a declaration reflects full membership, obligations, rights and membership of the European Union. This document sets out guidelines for the functioning of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia and the operational programme of the European Council, which our country, as a member of the European Union, must respect and ensure their legal placement and realization. Given that Slovenia is preparing for the Presidency of the European Union in 2008, it is necessary to ensure the timely adoption of all national harmonised acts, in agreement with the competent European institutions, as part of the preparations. In the Group of Members, we accept the structure of this document and its priorities, which are divided into three parts. The first part gives stronger political priorities and three components of Slovenia as an active partner in the European Union, development reforms and Slovenia, a reliable partner in the region and in the world. The scope of the second part sets out the priorities in the 17 chapters, in terms of upgrading the general policy orientations already presented in the first part. That is why European priorities should be included in this year, to the greatest extent possible, as part of political, economic, economic, social and environmental visions, and should also be addressed in a timely manner. In our Group of Members, we note that the priorities include, in addition to an extremely demanding project of the Presidency of the European Union, the adoption of the euro, the regulation of the Schengen border and, more recently, the increasingly deliberate labour market within the framework of the free movement within the Lisbon Strategy. At the same time, we ensure the proper promotion of our country in the European Union and in the European Belt at all. Slovenia will support the proper planning of the future financial perspective, the enlargement process of the European Union, the effective withdrawal of funds from the Structural Funds, support to the Western Balkan countries, the preservation and expansion of the role of the European Union at the global level. Under Chapter 6, greater attention will be paid to transparency of public finances, the promotion of longer-term employment in the labour market and pension insurance. At the same time, our party supports and advocates a model of lifelong learning and training that ensures the inclusion of the elderly in accordance with the provisions of the European Constitutional Treaty. The guidelines on improving the system and transferring the rights contained in the supplement to pension insurance and the active monitoring of demographic changes and their immediate consequences, and the timely adaptation of the policy of the protection of older persons and social protection policies, are extremely important. In the DeSUS Group, we stress social cohesion in terms of achieving faster development and meaningful measures for greater prosperity for citizens, more accessible to the E-government, E-Health, E-Business, etc. We note that the active involvement of individual ministries is needed to achieve a greater synergy effect with the possibility of checking and implementing harmonisation and liberalisation. All that is said is only part of a comprehensive document that represents, if we shrink, our position and the content of our operation in the European Community. Since the declaration with the amendments of the working bodies has been further improved, there is no reason not to support it in our parliamentary group, as it is in fact only a general framework within which the detailed content will always be determined by the sectoral legislation that is already in force and also by the one that is still coming. Thank you very much.

The declaration we are addressing today, through the priorities that are primarily the preparations for the Presidency of Slovenia in 2008, the continuation of preparations for the adoption of the euro, the implementation of the Lisbon Strategy, the revision of the EU's sustainable development, preparations for the next financial perspective, support for the further enlargement and approximation of the EU's Western Balkans and efforts to further strengthen the EU in the global international community, places us in a group of countries that have realised that it is not good to miss the opportunity, but we must do so on the understanding that we must create them primarily ourselves. In this context, in addition to preparing for the EU Presidency in the first half of 2008 and preparing for Slovenia's entry into the Schengen area, the most important task of taking over the euro is to take over the euro as of 1 January 2007. Despite some of the concerns that may arise in this context, the orientation of the Slovenian Government, which is a timely proposal for double price labelling adopted by this National Assembly and other measures, has provided the appropriate basis for preventing an unjustified price increase, has proved to be good. If there are no unexpected circumstances, we hope that Slovenia will be able to record the softest transition to the euro area, which should also allow for the introduction of the euro envisaged by the declaration and other legislative and technical adjustments envisaged and, of course, to inform the public of the exchange of currency. The other priorities, which are important not only for 2006 but also for our future development, are, of course, the reforms for development and, in this context, the implementation of the Lisbon Strategy, where Slovenia will strive to create an environment that is conducive to knowledge, research, education. It will work to remove barriers to worker mobility and create a better regulatory environment at European level that will facilitate business operations and the lives of citizens. We believe that this is one of the most important priorities, as well as the efficient use of resources and preparation for a new financial perspective, where priority activities stem from the adoption of regulations and other cohesion policy strategic documents, and the preparation of relevant domestic implementing documents in order to reduce developmental development between regions. The Declaration focuses on measures that will make the best possible use of the opportunities offered by the financial perspective adopted for the period 2007-2013 at the end of last year. Some discussions on the proposal for a declaration at the working bodies of the National Assembly also convince us that we are also able to face serious development issues and not just formal discussions about whether we are being brought more by pigeons or sparrows on the roof. These were some of the discussions last year when the Luxembourg proposal for a future financial perspective was not adopted last June, which was later shown not to be as favourable as some of them wanted to present to us in this meeting. Above all, it was significantly worse than the British proposal, which, as I have already said, was adopted at the end of last year. Slovenia has the most favourable position at any time in history following this proposal. We therefore believe that it is right to focus here in Parliament on discussions on how to improve the EU's use of funds. The European Union's Committee on Affairs has thus found that the situation in this area in 2004 has been poor, as we have heard today from the period 2004-2006, could have been disastrous. Actually, we didn't take advantage of anything. However, we have found that the situation has improved significantly in 2005, after the Government of the Republic of Slovenia adopted a number of operational measures last March, and that we are now achieving the best percentage of EU funds among the new members. Of course, we must not blind ourselves or be satisfied with what has been achieved. There is still a lot of nonsense and unnecessary bureaucracy in this area, but it is pleased to see the direction of the declaration of measures for rational and efficient use of funds. In this respect, the national development programme and the preparation of an appropriate set of quality projects for the next financial period are of exceptional importance. Of course, the national development programme should not be based solely on existing baselines, but on the past, it should also include good proposals for individual environments with a view to reducing the development backlog of individual less developed regions. There is no doubt that our country will be as developed as it will be to the least developed part. As the last of these three priorities, a stronger priority, Slovenia's reliable partner in the region and the world implies continued support for the enlargement process and support for the Western Balkan countries, support for the integration of Croatia and Turkey, stability and development of the Western Balkans, strengthening the Union as a global strategic partner to support the stability and development of the international community. In the second part of the declaration, where the broader priorities of Slovenia for the work of the Council of the European Union in 2006 are set out in more detail, we are following a series of guidelines, which we have heard a lot about today, so allow me to mention only some of the 17 chapters or of these key substantive definitions, which are far from the most important, however. It is cohesion policy, the implementation of the Lisbon Strategy, the general economic framework of policies, the strengthening of competitiveness, the development of an active internal market, the information society, energy, transport and policy towards chemicals, employment, social affairs and gender equality, the environment, the sustainable development of the common agricultural policy and fisheries, the areas of freedom, security and justice and the enlargement of the European Union. Your Honor! The 2006 Declaration on Guidelines for the Functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the Institutions of the European Union represents the basic orientation of our country in the further development of all processes, some of which we have become part of since 1 May 2004. During this period, Slovenia has demonstrated and proved that it is not only a credible member and partner, but has shown that it is able to participate effectively in the design of processes and decisions with a common objective of faster development and prosperity. The orientations of the declaration also prevent us from having specific priorities that have been politically unnecessaryly politicised, in fact, stem from guidelines that have already been endorsed or emanated from the common orientations of the European Union in the past. I am thinking, of course, of the changes in the reform package and others. Slovenia will be as much a European country as the standards in force in Europe will be able to apply on domestic soils. There is no reason for us not to be even better and more successful than the best in Europe. This does not depend solely on the government and the ruling parties but on everything between us. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we therefore want to see a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union in 2006 adopted by consensus in this High Council. We believe that this would be the best journey for all who will take care of its realization. Thank you very much.

Slovenia supports the adoption of the Services Directive as soon as possible, as we believe that liberalisation in the field of services in the market of twenty-five represents an exceptional opportunity for economic growth, for new jobs in the framework of twenty-five. This is why Slovenia supports, in particular, the basic text, which, unfortunately, has been quite recently amended in the European Parliament. However, respect for the position of the European Parliament must be respected and it is therefore a very difficult task for the Commission to make a text in the spirit of Lisbon from the basic proposal, the basic concept of the Services Directive and then the amendments adopted. This will be a significant task and Slovenia has the same position here as it has: we support the Services Directive in the basic text, with the exception of health services, social services and gambling. These exceptions are extended by amendments. We believe that the extension of exceptions is not even as problematic as it is mainly the position on the principle of the country of origin. According to Slovenia's position, the principle of the country of origin is in fact a foundation, a key point of the directive itself, and we therefore consider that deleting this principle from the directive would be a major substantive loss for the purpose of the directive itself. Slovenia also supports provisions for the supervision of posted workers and effective administrative cooperation, as well as provisions to ensure that the directive is neutral with regard to workers' protection legislation. In short, I think that the last amendments of the European Parliament are going a little bit towards easing or, I would say, away from the relatively ambitious objective of the original proposal. Slovenia is therefore in the position to take these solutions into account as much as possible in the final text as possible in the final text, as well as in Parliament's presented and debated solutions related to the Services Directive.

In view of the fact that it has been mentioned several times in the debate, I would like to briefly explain what we have done so far in this area so that we do not, in fact, open up questions where they are not needed. Of course, we have followed the experience of all Member States that have found that the first presidency requires a three-year preparation period. That is why we started preparations for the presidency at the beginning of last year. First, we set up the organisational structure of the Presidency, we set up a narrower working group led by the Prime Minister and sets out the general guidelines, then a narrower working group appointed a wider working group led by the chairman of the WHO and coordinated the area of five working groups. These working groups, however, cover the substantive programme, staff, logistics, communication with the public and promotion and the budget. On the subject of substantive preparations. The question has been asked several times. A first review of the current contents was prepared. These are the content that is expected to be on the European Agenda in 2008. Among these topics, which are few, the working group has already identified a narrower set of topics to which we intend to give a little more emphasis. These are the future of the European Union and institutional development, enlargement and the Western Balkans, the policy of the new neighbourhood and energy and intercultural dialogue. Of course, we need to know that the priorities will change in line with what is happening in the European Union, so it is difficult to say that we will remain in this. We're keeping an eye on it. However, the final decision on this will only be taken before the start of the Presidency. Further, with regard to substantive preparations. We have prepared a draft calendar of main events. This calendar will be updated and coordinated on a regular basis in the light of all the obligations we will have as Chair State. The prerequisite for the implementation of the proposed substantive programme of the Presidency is a well-thought-out work plan. Further, about the content. We are preparing an 18-month Presidency programme with Germany and Portugal. Of course, we are also preparing to coordinate this programme with France and the other other group's chairing countries. As far as training is concerned, the government has adopted a human resources plan for the presidency. The recruitment process for the presidency of Ljubljana and abroad is already in the final stage. We are now preparing a training programme for the selected candidates. We will continue our efforts in logistics this year. The government has therefore adopted a decision on the construction of a new conference centre in Brdo, which is urgently needed for the smooth implementation of informal meetings and meetings. Then we also pay attention to other aspects of logistics, namely the organisation of events, transports, security, document management, technical and information support, translation and so on, not to mention. Slovenia will also pay particular attention in 2006 to communication with the public and prepare in the field of promotion, because we are aware of the opportunity offered by the Presidency. We have therefore also drawn up a proposal for general promotion and cultural activities for the objectives of effective promotion of Slovenia during the Presidency, and this proposal is already in the final stage of coordination. As far as the Presidency is concerned, we are active in all areas, and in this way we will continue to do so. Thank you very much.

Article 99 contains a series of restrictions and conditions for such action, for example, that there must already be concrete evidence that a specific person is planning or already committing a number of very serious crimes, or that a particular person may be expected to commit extremely serious crimes in the future due to his past crimes. Further, such action shall be admissible only if this is permitted by national legislation on the basis of the request and approval of the competent authorities and where there is concrete evidence that such information is necessary to prevent a significant threat or other significant threat to the internal or external security of the State. PERIOD OF THE DAY - REGULAR DECISION ON THE LAW ON THE ELIMINATION OF THE IMPACT OF THE ACT WITH ASbestos. The total time for discussion is 4 hours and 25 minutes. At its 12th meeting, on 9 March 2006, the National Council of the Republic of Slovenia decided that, pursuant to the third indent of Article 97(1) of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, it would require the national assembly to re-judge before declaring the law to remedy the consequences of working with asbestos. I give the floor to the representative of the state council, Mrs. Marti Turk. I'd like you to do this from outside the pay phone. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u66	Thank you so much for saying that. On 9 March, as is already well known, at its 12th extraordinary session, the Council of State unanimously voted the procrastination veto on the law to remedy the consequences of working with asbestos. He thus warned that this law solves the problem of asbestos patients in Slovenia unilaterally, not comprehensively, and not as imaginatively as it could be if the legislator took more time to talk, both with the medical profession and with the affected people, with the patients due to asbestos, and with their relatives. The National Assembly now has another opportunity to supplement the law with provisions that will solve the problem of all asbestos patients, including the surrounding population, not just workers working with asbestos. I must strongly oppose the statements made by some of the representatives of the proposer and the coalition Members that could be heard by the Council of State with a suspended veto on the law on the elimination of the consequences of asbestos work, which makes it impossible or at least stops the resolution of the asbestos problem in Slovenia. Such an attitude does not correspond to the actual situation. On the contrary. It is time to take responsibility for the people and the environment in which we live by supplementing the law and, of course, to resolve this matter in a way that is right and good for all who are affected. The law of which the National Assembly will re-conclude today does not take into account the settlement of damage to the destroyed health of the population, which in the vicinity of asbestos cement production has been inhaling asbestos or coming into direct contact with it, does not provide social security for asbestos patients who are deprived of their rights, thus also creating a difference between the patients who have been sick for the same reason that I do not speak of affected individuals, mostly children with disabilities. The previous law also granted wider rights to people who actually lived in an area where asbestos was being processed, and this was the case with the leaders of both commissions of the State Council, who had repeatedly warned the applicants to include this problem in the proposal for a new law. ParlaMint-SI_2006-03-28-SDZ4-Redna-15.ana.u82	Thank you. Dear Vice-President, Honourable Minister, Honourable Director, Honourable Representative of the Council of State, Dear Fellows and Fellows, The Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group will once again support the law on the elimination of the consequences of asbestos because the system, comprehensive, consistent, artistic and socially responsible regulation of asbestos issues, both from the point of view of the injured, the companies and the state, as well as the foreseeable and assessed needs and requirements of the injured and the budgetary capacity. The new law is based on good practice that has been established in the European Union countries and has good or even better solutions in comparison to this practice for people who have suffered the consequences of working with asbestos as well as for economic societies. It sets out in its terms very clearly the occupational diseases and conditions for their identification, dosage and payment of lump sum compensation and the right to a disability pension under more favourable conditions in persons who have been identified as having been exposed to asbestos, i.e. persons who have been employed in companies that have processed, stored, implanted or removed asbestos or asbestos products. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group, we have always supported the proposal of the law on the abolition of asbestos work, because we believe that this law builds on the law on the prohibition of production and transport of asbestos products and on the provision of funds for the restructuring of asbestos production in asbestos, which was adopted in 1996 and was subsequently amended and supplemented three more times. The very titles of both laws show the basic purposes and objectives of these laws. The current law prohibited production and earmarked financial means for restructuring, while the new law provided social security for workers employed in companies where they received occupational diseases and for exposure to asbestos, and State aid to companies or at least 60% of the contribution to compensation. The main argument of the State Council that the new law does not include a category of people exposed to asbestos dust is therefore not in the context of the basic purpose of the new law, which eliminates the consequences of working with asbestos. Moreover, the State Council’s claim that the current law also granted wider rights to residents living near companies that processed or stored asbestos or asbestos products is not true, since in Article 1, it merely declared the provision of health and social security to the population. In other provisions, he did not mention the inhabitants at all. It only granted rights to workers employed in companies, but not to the surrounding population. According to data, no citizen in Slovenia has been granted rights under the law. The State Council also considers that the early retirement of workers with a occupational disease who obtain the status of second and third categories of disability must not be linked to the condition of 25 years of retirement and 50 years of age. We also support in our parliamentary group the asbestos issue of disability pension, which is precisely defined in the law on pension and disability insurance. In particular, the basic principle of fairness and equal treatment of all disabled persons who are recognised as Category I disability without all conditions and additional restrictions. Insured persons with recognised disability II. and III. the categories and additional conditions mentioned above, the age of 50 years and 25 years of retirement, which means that it is still much more favourable than those of all other disabled persons subject to general pension conditions. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group, we are unfavorably surprised by the lack of confidence of the State Council in the interdisciplinary group of experts and its appointment and the lack of confidence in the Interdepartmental Commission, which is demonstrated by the reason that representatives of all interested public should also be present in the implementation of such an important law and that there is no legal protection at the second stage against the opinion of the Commission, and that judicial protection is also excluded. The new law sets out very clearly the tasks of the Interdisciplinary Commission of Experts and the Interdepartmental Commission. In identifying the disease due to the consequences of asbestos work, an interdisciplinary group of top experts with appropriate medical knowledge shall perform the professional tasks. The Interdisciplinary Commission shall be appointed by the Minister of Health from among doctors involved in asbestos diseases. The Interdisciplinary Group shall deliver an opinion which shall be binding only on the decision of the Inter-Ministerial Commission in order to offer a settlement. If the interdisciplinary group does not identify a professional disease in the injured party, it still has the possibility to bring a dispute before a court where the existence of the disease and other consequences of working with asbestos can be demonstrated by judicial experts. It should be pointed out that when it comes to medical diagnosis and expert opinions, these are not questions that can be resolved by administrative procedure, but rather by highly professional and demanding work, in accordance with medical doctrine and medical ethics. On their legal nature, the expert opinions are preliminary questions on which the decisions of the competent authorities are based. In cases where necessary, the Interdepartmental Commission may invite the beneficiary or his legal representative to a meeting. Since it is a matter of exercising personal rights, the Ombudsman chooses his legal representative and cannot simply assume this role on the basis of law by one of the organisations affected by asbestos. If the law provided for an individual to be represented by an organisation in the Interdepartmental Commission, they would intervene in his freedom to decide with which organisation he would cooperate or who would represent his interests. The rights under this law are granted only by the beneficiaries on the basis of a settlement. The right to compensation or on the basis of a decision of SPIZ, a pension under more favourable conditions. If an individual disagrees with the attached settlement on compensation, the provision of a professional disease or if the competent interdisciplinary commission has not established a professional disease in his case, he may bring an action before a court. All legal remedies shall be available in this dispute before the court. It can be concluded from the above that the new law specifies the rights which may be exercised by injured workers, which is not covered by the law in force so far and which have been paid out on the basis of additional arrangements and without the rules formally published. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group, we have been actively involved in the substantive search for solutions to the bill, both with the asbestos patients' union, the asbestos society, with representatives of companies, with affected nationals, with coalition parties, and with government lawmakers. There has been a lot of reflection and discussion in our parliamentary group on how to include the surrounding population in the law, but this has not been justified on the basis of professional grounds, with concrete criteria regarding the distance to the asbestos production centre, nor on these grounds has it been possible to assess the potential number of patients with asbestos diseases in the coming years and decades, so as to establish an area to be professionally justified, in particular fair and fair to all the population. What are we going to tell those people who are going to stay behind the border of a certain area who are going to be sick of asbestos disease? Here lies our view of injustice and inequality before the law. So it is in itself a solution to extend the area to the entire territory of Slovenia. In this case, however, we are faced with a new dilemma there is no stranger. What about the remaining polluted and poisoned environments that are and also cause various physical and mental disorders, including the consequences of death. These are dilemmas and ignorances that have not even been touched by the law so far and have not been tried by the previous government and coalition, so a gradual, professional and responsible approach is needed, which means that this complex problem must be addressed by the consequences of the polluted and poisoned environment that causes various serious diseases of the population, first by the profession and then by the legislative branch of authority. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group, we believe that it is possible in the future to include the most affected surrounding population with appropriate additions, of course, if this has been done within the framework of constitutional equality before the law. We are more inclined to come up with a solution to draft a law on the elimination of the effects of pollution and the burden of the environment on asbestos or asbestos products. And it would be useful to include the inhabitants in this law. This would mean a third step in solving the asbestos problem in the Republic of Slovenia. Our Group of Members will always support any initiatives and solutions in this direction constructively, particularly state-level wisdom and responsibility. However, it will not support cheap populist debates and proposals that are not realistic and constitute a leap into the unknown. In our parliamentary group, we believe that the law on the elimination of the consequences of working with asbestos, the upgrading of the law so far, and that it is an appropriate legal basis for the long-term resolution of asbestos problems, as well as the legal basis for the implementation of dedicated budgetary spending. Failure to confirm this law would seriously endanger the social security of workers who have received occupational diseases due to asbestos exposure for an indefinite period of time. However, the Slovenian Democratic Party's parliamentary group does not want to take on this irresponsibility, so the law on the elimination of the consequences of asbestos work will once again be supported. Thank you for your attention.
